Person-to-Person Astrology: Energy Factors in Love, Sex and Compatibility

Appendix A

Astrology: A Language of Energy
 Defining the Essential Factors

If one is going to emphasize the energy approach to astrology and therefore the importance of the four elements, the following definitions—which I have used and found extremely accurate for many years—are worth keeping in mind. These definitions also focus on astrology as a language of personal
experience
, as contrasted with the old-fashioned attempt to squeeze a description of outer events out of every astrological pattern.

The
elements
 are the
energy substance of experience
.

The
signs
 are the
primary energy patterns
 and indicate specific
qualities of experience
.

The
planets

regulate energy flow
 and represent the
dimensions of experience
.

The
houses
 represent the
fields of experience
 wherein specific energies will be
most easily expressed
 and
most directly encountered
.

The
aspects
 reveal the
dynamism and intensity of experience
 as well as
how the energies within the individual interact
.

These five factors, defined and understood as stated above, constitute a remarkably comprehensive, sophisticated, and refined language of energy and cosmic psychology. These factors are combined in the following way: A particular dimension of experience (indicated by a certain planet) will invariably be colored by the quality of the sign wherein it is placed in the individual’s chart. This combination results
in a
specific urge toward self-expression
 and a particular
need for fulfillment
. The individual will confront that dimension of life most immediately in the field of experience indicated by the planet’s house position. And, although the urge to express or fulfill that dimension of experience will be present in anyone having a certain planet-sign combination, the specific aspects to that planet reveal how easily and harmoniously the person can express that urge or fulfill that need.

Appendix B

Chart Calculation Services

It is essential to have accurate data. You will need the date, location (city and country), and the exact time of birth. Mothers and fathers often have fuzzy memories about the exact time of birth, so it is advisable to obtain a copy of the birth certificate for the person if there is any question. If born in the USA and the county where you were born is known, you can most likely order birth certificates online at the county’s website. If you don’t know, you might try
www.vitalrec.com
 for help.

Many websites and calculation services adjust for daylight savings time, “War Time,” and the details of time zones’ complications, but it’s a good idea to double-check all data before entering. One wrong number in the date or time changes everything!

Here are a few reliable, inexpensive or free online sites where one can get birth charts calculated:

Astrocom.com
 is Astro Communications Services’ website. On their homepage, click on “Charts” (under “Products”). You can order a “One-Ring Student Chart” with just the 10 planets (less confusing!) for $4.95 online, or you place an order by telephone or ask questions about the data in question by calling 800-514-5070.

For free chart calculation:

Astrolabe’s website,
alabe.com
, offers free charts. They give clear, basic planets’ positions and house cusps to the nearest degree with a short paragraph of interpretation (sometimes with conflicting information,
as happens often in astrology software interpretation programs). Recommended for beginners since graphics, etc., are easily understood.

Astro.com
, Astrodienst’s website, allows you to save multiple charts if you register. Go to “Free Horoscopes.” Click on “Chart drawing, Ascendant” for basic calculation. Calculations seem very good, but extended options can be confusing.

Author’s note:
 Simpler, clear charts are the best for beginners. House system issues, minor aspects and planetary bodies, and so on, should be ignored by beginners. The contradicting and lame “interpretations,” “personal portraits,” “love charts,” etc., that are often offered with free calculation are often an impediment to learning astrological basics. They are trying to sell you stuff!

Warning:
 Some sites require you to give your e-mail address, and you may be inundated with spam, offers for psychic readings, “special offers,” etc., as a consequence. Remember that many people running “astrological websites” have no real knowledge of astrology and are simply predators on gullible people.

Appendix C

Moon Sign Tables

Of all the planets in a birth chart, the Moon moves the most rapidly. Hence, creating any table to estimate its sign position is—at best—only a way of portraying the Moon sign’s two possibilities for those born on a certain date. Because the Moon moves into a new sign approximately every two and a half days, there is no alternative but to get an accurately calculated birth chart if one wants to know for certain his or her Moon’s placement. In addition, once one knows the exact degree of the Moon in a particular chart, it is possible to experiment with aspects between that Moon and another person’s planets—a very important and illuminating technique described in
Chapters 7
 and
17
.

To find your Moon sign, check under the year and month of your birth and then scan that line of Moon placements. If the actual date of your birth is not listed, refer to the date before your birthdate. That sign is most likely to be your Moon’s sign location. If that does not fit your emotional nature, then the following sign is likely to be your Moon sign. Refer to my descriptions in
Chapter 7
 to compare the qualities and psychological tendencies of the two possible Moon signs.

Appendix D

Venus and Mars Sign Tables

The following tables should be reliable for determining one’s Venus and Mars signs, except during the two days when the planet is changing signs. A precisely calculated chart will be needed to be sure of the planets’ sign placements. And, in any case, the exact degree of each planet’s position will be needed if one is to experiment accurately with “aspects” between two charts.

Person-to-Person Astrology: Energy Factors in Love, Sex and Compatibility

Appendix E

Tables for Estimating the
 Ascendant (or Rising Sign)

Determining the
exact degree
 of the zodiac that was “rising” on the eastern horizon for any individual at the moment of birth requires a precise calculation based not only on an accurate birth time (preferably from a written record rather than from the mother’s memory), but also on knowing the correct time zone and whether summer time or another daylight savings time plan was in effect. However, the following tables will in most cases enable a person to make a reasonable estimate of the zodiac sign that was “ascending” or rising, or to determine the most likely two rising signs from which the one that seems most accurate can be chosen. (Tables are adapted from
Star Signs for Lovers
, courtesy of Stein & Day, Inc., publishers, via CRCS Publications, exclusive hardcover edition distributor.)

Note:
 If you were born during daylight savings time or during World War II, be sure to subtract one hour from your birth time before using these tables to estimate your Ascendant.

Example:
 If you were born January 3 at 9 a.m., the tables from January 1 to 7 will show you that your Ascendant is most likely between 7 and 15 degrees of Aquarius. Assuming your birth time is precisely 9 a.m. (and not rounded off by the person who recorded it!), your Ascendant is then approximately 9 to 10 degrees Aquarius.

Please note that using the following tables will be only a quick guess, because time zone, wartime, and daylight savings time factors complicate the issue and have to be closely checked through a competent calculation service or program to have total confidence in the precise Ascendant at birth.

Person-to-Person Astrology: Energy Factors in Love, Sex and Compatibility

About the Author

Stephen Arroyo’s eight books have made him one of the best-selling authors of modern astrology books worldwide. Among his works are
Astrology, Psychology & the Four Elements; Astrology, Karma & Transformation; Relationships and Life Cycles; Stephen Arroyo’s Chart Interpretation Handbook;
 and
Exploring Jupiter
. His writings have elicited such tremendously enthusiastic response internationally that they have now been translated into twenty languages, and he has been awarded the British Astrological Association Astrology Prize, the Fraternity of Canadian Astrologers’ International Sun Award, and United Astrology Congress’s Regulus Award.

Arroyo’s work spans the spectrum of modern, psychologically oriented astrology, supported by his many years of one-to-one counseling work (he holds a California Marriage, Family, and Child Counseling license), and body- and energy-centered therapies such as Polarity Therapy. With a background in mathematics and the arts (an honors degree in literature from the University of California), and a master’s degree in psychology, Stephen Arroyo has developed a system in which the individual’s
experience
 is foremost, disregarding the fatalism and negativity of old-fashioned astrology. His writings are renowned for the reliable methods he has distilled for understanding astrological principles in relation to the human energy field, in a way that people of all cultures and backgrounds can easily relate to. As
Library Journal
 stated, his work is particularly notable for his unique ability to express complex concepts in clear, simple language that the reader can identify with and apply in his or her own life.

In addition to numerous appearances in the USA, Arroyo has been a guest speaker or seminar leader in the UK, Spain, France, Italy, Mexico, and Switzerland. He has taught some of the first college courses on astrology in the USA, at four different schools.

=== OEBPS/Arro_9781556439735_epub_c01_r1.htm ===
Person-to-Person Astrology: Energy Factors in Love, Sex and Compatibility

1

Compatibility and the Language of Relationships

The meeting of two personalities is like the contact of two chemical substances: if there is any reaction, both are transformed.

—C. G. Jung, Psychologist

In this book, I take the approach that the dynamic interaction of two people is describable and, to a great extent, understandable through the language of astrology. Many readers have heard of terms like “sexual chemistry” or have even said to a friend about a new relationship: “We just don’t have any chemistry.” Although I am of course not going to analyze relationships on the basis of physical chemistry, I do take a rather parallel approach to understanding interpersonal dynamics: there are interactions, catalysts, mutually affecting interchanges, transformations, and sometimes explosions.

However, rather than using chemical terminology, I have come to use the language of
energy
 in a way that is somewhat similar to how it is increasingly used in many innovative branches of the healing arts in recent years. In short, when considering any close relationship, I focus on each individual’s need for what I might call
energy nourishment
, as well as on how the two people “feed” or stimulate each other in certain ways, and deplete or block each other in other ways.

In addition, one of the most eye-opening and exciting experiences of my intellectual life was discovering that astrology’s ancient “four
elements” (Air, Fire, Water, and Earth) provide an amazingly accurate language to describe such energies and energy interactions in relationships. I especially liked the immediacy of the four elements’ significance when I began to appreciate their practical application. Not only did Air or Fire—as well as other symbols of the astrological language such as Venus or Mars—make immediate, almost tactile sense to me and confirm my emotional, physical, and energetic experience with other people; but they also appealed to me because they are transcultural, nonsexist, simple, and timeless (there have been systems of the elements, in a wide variety of cultures, for at least 3,000 years). The astrological symbols also have the great advantage of bypassing the increasingly politicized and confrontational language of recent Western popular culture and psychology that has made it more and more impossible to perceive the reality of one’s own private experience, especially for the “educated” segment of society. The multiple layers of contradictory concepts, biased psychological theories, and self-deceptions that have been heaped upon our emotional and sexual lives have created so many illusions, hypersensitivities, and resentments that clear perception of one’s own situation and needs is, for many, extremely difficult.

One thing missing from the vast majority of current writings and theories on the psychology of love, sex, and compatibility is the clear acknowledgment that people are innately different—immensely so—and therefore suited to vastly different lifestyles, learning styles, interests, duties, communication methods, emotional modes, and social roles. This failure to confront such an obvious fact of life is so gaping a void and so crucial an issue that an awareness of its importance reveals the complete lack of a solid theoretical foundation underlying many popular and academic psychological theories. Perhaps we should take the advice of the great philosopher and internationally renowned scientist Teilhard de Chardin and seek out a broader frame of reference
for human understanding. He asserted, “The human phenomenon must be measured on a cosmic scale.”

Although most academics and psychologists will not even consider using astrology as a model for understanding human nature, they should at least acknowledge that they have no accurate or scientific system that explains the profound and striking differences in human personality and behavior. Certainly, those who pose as “scientists” or journalists reporting on “recent scientific findings” should at least occasionally admit that “statistical truths” and the headline-making generalizations derived from them may in fact have very little accuracy
when applied to individual lives
. Such statements and media reports merely amplify the modern confusion, tension, worry, self-consciousness, and fear; and they often further expand the destructive polarization of the sexes.

Today we live in what an English friend of mine has called the “New Victorianism”—the opposite form of hypocrisy from the 1890s’ version. Instead of public reticence and private earthiness, we have today a widespread public flaunting of completely unaesthetic and often hideous and intentionally repulsive behavior, tastes, and desires. But behind that exhibitionistic media-fueled cultural frenzy lies a profound loneliness, insecurity, and increasing inability to relate to the opposite sex. This inability (in spite of endless books, courses, theories, and workshops on “relating”) has developed because, in many parts of Western societies, there are no solid social standards or clearly defined roles remaining today that enable one to have healthy contact with the opposite sex.

In fact, it is my observation that sex (isolated from an emotional or relationship context) is so overemphasized today in part because of the industrialized, urban society in which we live—we are all ungrounded in the earth! We have lost the broader contact with the body and senses associated with a healthy, natural lifestyle, and—for many—sex
remains the only contact we still have with the primal, physical life force and the world of nature. Today we hear endless and varied viewpoints about sex, such as:

Sex is chemistry generated between bodies

Sex develops from love

Sex is only an automatic and impersonal instinct

Sex is a skill to be learned—“try harder and you’ll do better”

Sex will be attracted if you have fame, power, or money

Sex will come to you if you spend enough money on clothes, makeup, and so on

There have been many astrological books published on “sex signs,” “love signs,” or the like; but actually, the Sun signs alone do not describe or explain an individual’s sexual or emotional nature. The solar system is obviously more complicated than just the Sun; consequently, from the viewpoint of astrology, the vast solar system of each person’s psychological nature is much more complex than just the Sun sign. As I will explore in this book, many other factors, especially Venus, Mars, and the Moon, must be included in any analysis of our relationship attunements or our cosmic psychology.

Much of this book is focused on the subject of “compatibility.” The most literal meaning of
compatibility
 derives from the Latin
cum passus
, meaning “stepping with” or “stepping together.” This is an image I especially like: two people, walking through life together in harmony or with a comfortable mutual rhythm, not necessarily looking to each other for all their satisfaction in life, but confronting life experiences together as they are encountered. This dynamic aspect of compatibility is reflected in the spontaneous acknowledgment of many contented couples when they simply say, “We just get along!”

The word compatibility also has a close relation to
compassion
, from the Latin
compati
, “to sympathize.” This aspect of the meaning of compatibility reminds me of the sympathetic resonance of two musical instruments, an accurate image of two people experiencing harmony in their energy exchange. In short, all aspects of my understanding of compatibility imply
action
—mutual movement while in accord, flowing with each other
on an energy level
, which also implies mutual support and a common direction in life.

I also should provide a bit of background for this book and the astrological language that I have developed over the years. When working with people one-to-one, I found that using
a simple language of experience
 to explain astrology (rather than the types of abstractions often found in astrology books) was something that people of all walks of life could readily understand. A typical week’s work in my counseling practice would include clients as diverse as a pear farmer, a Protestant minister, a housewife married to a rancher, a wandering professor’s wife raising children, a single male entrepreneur, an accountant, a librarian, a female student artist, and a chiropractor wanting to use astrology to understand his patients better. Over the years, I have had many professors, doctors, successful businesspeople, artists, and professionals of all sorts as clients. Some of the best students in my classes included a chemistry professor (who eventually helped teach how to calculate a birth chart in beginning classes), an engineer (who had to admit that astrology’s accurate analysis of compatibility was almost unbelievable), and an MD forensic pathologist (who was always interested in investigating the underlying causes of things). Most competent practitioners of astrology can also list a great many extremely accomplished people as regular clients or students, but naturally many of those enthusiastic beneficiaries of astrological insights prefer to keep a low profile due to the severely cynical and opinionated views of astrology that so many people hold.

Over almost four decades now, I have therefore developed a language for explaining astrology to a wide variety of people. This particular book has evolved into an in-depth study of just a few factors in astrology—especially Venus, Mars, and the Moon. This book is, in short, a summary of information synthesized from a much broader starting point. Because most students of astrology are women, the majority of the completed questionnaire replies were submitted by women. I recognized this potential distortion right away when starting the research for this book, and I have tried to balance that by specifically soliciting interviews with many men (and with some women who did
not
 know astrology, but whose birthdays I knew). In any case, the slight tilt toward the feminine viewpoint in many of the quotations can perhaps be considered a counterbalance to my own viewpoint.

Although most of the principles examined in this book can be applied to a variety of person-to-person relationships, my focus is specifically on the relatively intimate types of relationships between male and female. The scope of this volume does not include parent-child, teacher-student, employer-employee, or friendship relationships, although many aspects of such relationships can be better understood through an understanding of the principles and types of energy exchange examined in this book.

It is important to note that I’ll be discussing extensively various masculine, feminine, emotional, and behavioral
principles
—which should not be equated simply with the male or female nature or psychology. Astrology enables one to be much more specific than most psychological jargon when speaking of the relative masculine and feminine qualities and modes of expression that people exhibit, and the reader should use the astrological language with that precision and flexibility. Sexuality and love constitute such a continuum that I cannot possibly cover the entire range in this book. The complexity of just heterosexual person-to-person relationships is mind-boggling.
I do not attempt to include any special cases or nontraditional lifestyles as a major focus. The best writing and research always focuses on that with which one has personal familiarity and experience, and I have kept to this principle here. Although numerous clients were acknowledged homosexuals or were struggling with sexual issues or identity confusion, the vast majority of compatibility analyses (using comparisons of the two birth charts) that I have done have been for male-female couples.

For a more comprehensive understanding of any sort of relationship through the astrological lens, a full birth chart, calculated accurately for the precise time and place of birth, should be obtained. Sources for such an accurately calculated chart are included in
Appendix B
. (The tables in Appendices C, D, and E can be used to estimate the sign placements of some planetary bodies, but for an exact determination [especially of the precise sign of the Moon, which moves very quickly], the full mathematical calculation must be done, utilizing the most accurate birth time that one can find. The mother’s memory should not be trusted, unless no written record is available.)

The essential thing that I want the reader to realize and learn is that a fantastically insightful and accurate tool exists for self-understanding, which until now has been much neglected or skeptically dismissed. There exists today an astrological psychology (which I usually prefer to call “cosmic psychology” or “celestial psychology”) that has come of age and is of tremendous value to people of all walks of life and all levels of education. This “cosmic psychology” focuses on people’s
inner experience;
 and it also identifies the fluctuations of energy that people experience in life’s ups and downs, both on an individual level and in relationships. It studies individual
motivation
 and explores the amazing array of different types of consciousness that the infinite variety of human personalities exhibit.

Person-to-Person Astrology: Energy Factors in Love, Sex and Compatibility

Should I Really Take Astrology Seriously? Replies Throughout the Centuries

It is theory that decides what we can observe.

—Albert Einstein

In many ancient cultures, and also in modern ones more integrated with the rhythms of nature than is our own, the unity of human life with the celestial cycles and patterns in the sky would not even have to be discussed. It would be obvious to them that human life is part of the same unified reality of natural life. It would be taken for granted, and the assertion that we egotistical modern people are completely free to choose our life directions and outcomes—independent of all natural forces—would be considered ridiculous, naïve, or arrogantly foolish. And yet, when the subject of astrology is raised, no other field except religion (and perhaps politics) elicits such extreme and negative opinions from otherwise-sensible people who have absolutely no knowledge of the subject at hand. This rather pervasive bias is not new.

The numerous anti-astrology fanatics who today persist in ignorant tirades evidently have no respect even for the many eminent and brilliant people throughout history who have indeed found great value in studying astrology, as this chapter will show. In addition, those jumping on the anti-astrology bandwagon, whether “scientists” or religious fundamentalists, are suffering from the type of superstition
that they supposedly decry because they have allowed fear-induced
beliefs
 to substitute for
knowledge
. And a widespread popular bias about astrology being merely a silly amusement or part of the entertainment industry, as most “Sun sign” newspaper columns seem to confirm, is further encouraged by the flippant and irresponsible attitude taken by much of the media, who have no knowledge whatsoever on the subject.

I sometimes wonder what sort of arrogance can prompt so many modern people to express vehement opinions about astrology that contradict those of some of the greatest minds in history. Do such people really think they are the intellectual equals of Galileo, Kepler, Brahe, Descartes, Jung, Marcus Aurelius, Emerson, Bacon, Koestler, and Goethe? And I wonder what prompts people to betray so transparently their ignorance, prejudices, and fears? One explanation comes from Professor Zeldin, when he writes, “Again and again, apparently intelligent people ooze contempt to protect themselves from what they cannot understand, as animals defend their territory with foul smells” (IHH, p. 221).

Another possible explanation is that the modern ego has been so built up with pride and narrow-minded, materialistic “education” that many people actually feel that they are not part of the cosmos that surrounds them and gave birth to them. Instead, they believe they are completely independent beings with absolute freedom of will and total knowledge of everything significant. Self-delusion like that borders on a form of mental illness and is amazingly widespread in the Western world today. Such foolishness cannot but lead to lives of isolation, disillusionment, and alienation from nature, the universe,
and other people
.

This lamentable situation in the “educated” Western world is in stark contrast to that of many other eras—for example, the Renaissance in Europe. As Professor Anthony Grafton of Princeton University shows in his book,
Cardano’s Cosmos
, broadly educated sixteenth-century
thinkers (who knew multiple ancient and modern languages as well as philosophy, mathematics, and the arts) found in astrology much of what we now call psychology, political theory, astronomy, science, philosophy, sociology, and economics—all the fundamental tools they needed for analyzing themselves and understanding human nature and society.

In part, the disrepute in which astrology is held today grows from the division between the worlds of science and art, the quantitative (and thus measurable) and the qualitative. One of the first commentators to focus on that schism, and its negative effects on science and society, was the brilliant and multi-talented Arthur Koestler, whose writings encompass one of the broadest arrays of intellectual achievement in modern times: novels, international journalism in multiple languages, social-change and political essays, autobiography, and books about psychology, parapsychology, creativity theory, and the history of science. A person of rare intellectual and physical courage, his mid-twentieth-century novel
Darkness at Noon
 took the world by storm by revealing the reality of the Soviet communist system, when most artists, intellectuals, and many academics were sympathetic to it. Some of his later works, such as
The Act of Creation
 and
The Sleepwalkers: A History of Man’s Changing Vision of the Universe
, explore with remarkable objectivity and integrity many of the key issues of modern intellectual and scientific life, decades before most other people were aware of the problems.

Koestler wrote in
The Sleepwalkers
, “Yet all cosmological systems, from the Pythagoreans to Copernicus, Descartes and Eddington, reflect the unconscious prejudices, the philosophical or even political bias of their authors; and from physics to physiology, no branch of Science, ancient or modern, can boast freedom from metaphysical bias of one kind or another” (SWK, p. 11). That book focuses on what Koestler called a “comprehensive survey of man’s changing vision of the universe which encloses him.” I mention his work because it ties
in completely with the reason I used Einstein’s quotation at the beginning of this chapter: namely, that the theories we hold determine what we can observe or imagine. If our theories are too narrow or imbalanced, we are unnecessarily restricting what we can perceive about life! Conventional, narrow, materialistic views may enable us to fit in better with some of our peers and neighbors and prevent friction with some of our professional colleagues. But it severely limits our life, as well as both our intellectual and our personal development potential. One answer to the question “Why should I take astrology seriously?” is simply: in order to
see
 more.

Actually, even from a rigid materialistic point of view, much evidence supports the value of investigating astrology’s practical uses. As Deepak Chopra, MD, said in an interview, “The body is monitoring the movement of stars. Biological movements are a function of planetary movements—circadian, seasonal, etc.… There is an underlying intelligence that organizes the infinity of things happening in the universe and connects all things with each other” (San
Francisco Chronicle
, January 11, 1997). In fact, the narrow materialistic orientation is disintegrating in many fields of study. Environmental scientist William Keepin, Ph.D., examines in a
Life
 magazine article physicist David Bohm’s proposal that the universe is a single unbroken entity in flowing motion (called a
holomovement
), in which each part replicates the whole. The three basic manifestations of this entity are matter, energy, and meaning, and each enfolds the other two. Bohm’s vision was presaged by astrology’s ancient principle: “As above, so below.” In other words,
planets and people are part of a single process, bound together by patterns of meaning
. Astrology merely attempts to chart, monitor, or analyze those patterns. But because meaning cannot be quantified, astrology cannot be proved scientifically to the satisfaction of those who believe that everything real is measurable. But such an attitude is fruitless, because, as Keepin points out, “It’s like trying to prove the beauty of Mozart’s music using chemistry.”

It is particularly curious that Dr. Keepin uses the analogy of music, since a controversial book published by another scientist also uses a musical analogy to try to represent the way the solar system functions in relation to human beings, thus providing a potential explanation for astrology’s baffling accuracy. Dr. Percy Seymour, Principal Lecturer on Astronomy and Astrophysics at Plymouth University in England and also Researcher at the Greenwich Royal Observatory, caused such a strong reaction among his conservative colleagues with the publication of his book
Astrology: The Evidence of Science
 that the controversy was covered in an article in the
Times
 of London. The article included the following quote from his work: “… the whole solar system is playing a symphony on the Earth’s magnetic field.” Once again, in Dr. Seymour’s theory, correlations between the solar system and the individual are assessed in terms of vibration, harmony, and waves of energy. Dr. Seymour essentially describes the solar system as an intricate web of planetary fields and resonances.

The author of numerous books on astronomy, including
Cosmic Magnetism
, which won him international academic renown, Dr. Seymour holds doctorate degrees in astronomy and astrophysics and never had much interest in astrology until he was motivated to examine it when he heard it violently attacked or emotionally dismissed by scientific colleagues who usually pretended to be objective. As he stated in an interview, “So, having examined the arguments that supposedly disproved astrology, I came to the conclusion that they were totally unscientific—a form of rationalized bigotry cloaked in academic language” (TMM). Although his theories and hypotheses are too complex to present in detail here, they may well have cracked open the door to understanding some part of why astrology works so remarkably. Dr. Seymour’s ideas even include a speculation that there is a prenatal attunement to the magnetic fields of the surrounding solar system. A brief quote provides the reader a taste of his approach:

In the process of trying to formulate a mechanism to explain Gauquelin’s results [statistically valid studies on planetary positions and choice of profession], I was led to consider magnetobiology, a vast body of knowledge that links planetary alignments with magnetic events on the Sun and the biological consequences of fluctuations in Earth’s magnetic field. That’s what actually gave me the idea that there is a mechanism, magneto tidal resonance, which can not only explain the Sun’s magnetism, but also why specific fluctuations in Earth’s magnetosphere can get phase-locked with the tidal effects of the planets. (TMM)

In summary, the “weight of the evidence,” as they say in the legal trade, is getting to be overwhelming that broader new perspectives on life, science, human nature, and human potential must be accepted today in all fields of study and in all the helping professions. If in fact we are beginning to see, at least theoretically, the entire universe (or at least our solar system) as a whole, organized system of energies, rhythms, functions, and so forth, then it follows that every organic system within that greater whole bears some harmonic relation to the rest of the system. The ancient philosophers, sages, and astrologers in many cultures called this the relationship of the microcosm to the macrocosm.

What Do Emerson, Galileo, and da Vinci Have in Common?

I mentioned earlier the names of some of the greatest minds in history and their acknowledgment of finding value in astrology. Here I briefly quote their actual statements and/or actions related to astrology. Sir Francis Bacon (1561–1626), a contemporary of Shakespeare (1564–1616), is well known as one of the most brilliant people in recorded history, often equated with da Vinci in the breadth of his
work, knowledge, and originality. Sometimes called the “father of modern science,” Bacon chided astrologers for their abuse of the art but looked forward to the day when it would be established on a more rational basis, purged of its excesses and malpractice: “Astrology should rather be purged than absolutely rejected”
(Advancement of Learning
, World Classics, Vol. 1, p. 86). Bacon also wrote, “The natures and dispositions of men are, not without truth, distinguished from the predominances of the planets” (ibid.).

Johannes Kepler, whom astronomers revere for his discovery of the laws of planetary motion, showed a truly scientific attitude. Although he was at first skeptical about many aspects of astrology, he eventually reached the following conclusions:

An unfailing experience of mundane events in harmony with the changes occurring in the heavens has instructed and compelled my unwilling belief.… As experience teaches us, that most beautiful gift of God, that noble phenomenon of the divine orbits and their earthly influences (i.e. astrology), is brought in disrepute by nothing else as much as by the trend of some to claim too much for her through unseemly and superstitious prating, and thus drive the learned away from her. (Kepler,
Collected Works
, Munich, 1938)

I was especially pleased to see the last quote (from his writings in the year 1598!) because I have stated and written numerous times that it is often the astrologers who bring astrology into disrepute by claiming too much for it or treating it as a religion. Kepler in fact made it one of his life’s ambitions to place astrology on a more reliable and respectable footing. As he wrote, “Yet for such excesses, astrologers have nobody to blame but themselves. Not only have they through many abuses brought the salutary art into disrepute and suspicion, but they understood so little of its real worth, for which I here stand up” (ibid.).

The great Galileo (1564–1642) taught and practiced astrology for many decades, and he lectured to medical students about it at the
University of Padua in Italy. It was then considered an indispensable adjunct of medicine, and I dare say that it should be still. I personally know a number of doctors of medicine, chiropractic, psychology, and psychiatry who (usually secretly) use it to understand the nature and vitality of their patients and the cycles they are going through. It contributes immensely to efficiency since it enables the doctor to quickly understand the patient in great depth.

A renowned professor of physics (I do not want to jeopardize his career by stating his name) was quoted a few years ago as saying the following: “Mathematics has been and will continue to be a language of science, but astrology will become the qualitative language of the human condition.”

In the field of business management, I have also found a certain reluctance on the part of managers and CEOs to let others know that they use astrology or astrological consultants, and so I cannot mention many examples here nor divulge confidential information. However, Richard Jenrette, an Aries Sun who is too independent and too rich to care much about other people’s opinions, wrote extensively in his book
The Contrarian Manager
 about how useful he found astrology to be. Considering that Aries people are generally impatient and need to quickly grasp the essentials of situations and other people’s abilities, this seems to me an especially powerful testimonial. There is no doubt that an Aries Sun like Jenrette would not waste time on astrology if he did not find it immediately valuable. Jenrette was one of the founders of the extremely successful Wall Street firm Donaldson, Lufkin & Jenrette (or “DLJ”), a pioneer in small company research that was ultimately sold for many millions of dollars to another company. I have also recently been in touch with an expert in placing executives in the pharmaceutical field who uses astrology in order to fit the person to the situation and to the specific management skills required.

The inspiring American philosopher Ralph Waldo Emerson concisely defined astrology as follows: “Astrology is astronomy brought
to earth and applied to the affairs of men.” This quotation seems to be an almost exact parallel to psychologist Ralph Metzner’s statement that astrology is “astronomy applied for psychological purposes” (from Dr. Metzner’s article “Potential Science and Intuitive Art”), especially because the term “psychological” was unheard of in Emerson’s time.

In the literary field, I could quote hundreds of examples, but I will limit myself here to quotes from only two. The great German writer Goethe wrote, “Astrology has its beginning in a remote sense of some great cosmic unity.” And the controversial and pioneering American writer Henry Miller, who made insightful and appreciative comments about astrology in a number of his books, explained: “Astrology does not offer an explanation of the laws of the universe, nor why the universe exists. What it does, to put it in simplest terms, is to show us that there is a rhythm to the universe, and that man’s own life partakes of this rhythm” (Miller,
Wisdom of the Heart
, NY: New Directions, 1960).

Among modern sciences, it is no doubt within the field of psychology that astrology has been most extensively used, tested, and accepted. This is not surprising since astrology is probably most precisely accurate and useful when applied to understanding the varieties of human personality and motivation. The following quotations give a taste of the broad scope of practical uses that astrology can provide to psychologically oriented people.

Psychology textbooks of future generations will look back on modern psychologists working without the aid of astrology as being like medieval astronomers working without the aid of the telescope. (Richard Tarnas, Ph.D., Professor of Psychology, California Institute of Integral Studies; Author of
The Passion of the Western Mind
 and
Cosmos and Psyche: Intimations of a New World View
)

Astrology offers, first of all, a personality system based on an external frame of reference which is therefore superior to the arbitrary
systems manufactured in such abundance within the field of personality study, and which is almost certain to be the universal system of the psychology of the future. It offers a symbolic blueprint of a human mind … which cannot be manipulated by the subject wishing to “fake good” or “fake bad” as it is relatively easy to do in many psychological questionnaires. It offers insight into areas of which the subject often knows little or nothing … repressions, values never consciously verbalized, ambivalences and conflicts projected into events and relationships and never consciously faced. It offers clues to unrealized potentials, talents, natural channels for integration and sublimation, etc.… It permits the “matching” of individuals, from therapist to patient to marriage partners to employee-employer, etc. It is my firm conviction that the psychotherapy or counseling of the future will use the horoscope as routinely as we now use the interview and background data on the subject. (Zipporah Dobyns, Ph.D., Psychology, “Astrology as a Psychological Tool,”
Aquarian Agent
 magazine, 1970)

Another psychologist, who did important work at Harvard University and whose publications include
Maps of Consciousness
, Ralph Metzner, Ph.D., concisely but comprehensively explains what astrology can contribute to psychology and to self-understanding:

As a psychologist and psychotherapist, I have been interested in another aspect of this baffling and fascinating subject. We have here a psychological typology and diagnostic assessment device far exceeding in complexity and sophistication of analysis any existing system … the framework of analysis—the three interlocking symbolic alphabets of zodiac “signs,” “houses,” and “planetary aspects”—is probably better adapted to the complex varieties of human natures than the existing systems of types, traits, motives, needs, factors, or scales.

The system has the additional advantage of being entirely independent of any behavior on the part of the subject, hence free of any response bias of any sort.… Unlike any other personality assessment device, the astrological pattern has an inherent dynamic: the horoscope interpreted by a skilled and practiced astrologer not only provides a synthetic picture of a person’s hereditary inclinations and tendencies, but points to latent potentials, suggests directions of needed growth—in short, gives a symbolic map of the process of self-realization. (Metzner, 1970, pp. 164–165)

Stanislav Grof, MD, a psychiatrist who has written incisively about astrology in such books as
Ancient Wisdom and Modern Science
, was Chief of Psychiatric Research at the Maryland Psychiatric Research Center and Assistant Professor of Psychiatry at Johns Hopkins University School of Medicine. He has this to say about astrology’s value:

Astrology is in my opinion the only system that can successfully predict both the content and timing of experiences encountered in non-ordinary states of consciousness in experiential psychotherapy. (Grof,
Ancient Wisdom & Modern Science
, NY: Suny Press, 1984)

Astrology, a discipline rejected and ridiculed by Newtonian-Cartesian science, can prove of unusual value as a source of information about personality development and transformation.… For an approach that sees consciousness as a primary element of the universe that is woven into the very fabric of existence, and that recognizes archetypal structures as something that precedes and determines phenomena in the material world, the function of astrology would appear quite logical. (ibid.)

The eminent scholar and psychiatrist C. G. Jung was one of the first psychological therapists to use astrology for a better understanding of
the inner dynamics of their clients. Jung acknowledged that he used astrology in many of his cases, especially with those people whom he had difficulty understanding.

As I am a psychologist, I’m chiefly interested in the particular light the horoscope sheds on certain complications in the character. In cases of difficult psychological diagnosis I usually get a horoscope in order to have a further point of view from an entirely different angle. I must say I have very often found that the astrological data elucidated certain points which I otherwise would have been unable to understand. (from Jung’s letter to Prof. B. V. Raman, September 6, 1947)

In an interview with the editor of a French astrological magazine, Jung (1954) stated:

One can expect with considerable assurance that a given well-defined psychological situation will be accompanied by an analogous astrological configuration. Astrology consists of configurations symbolic of the collective unconscious which is the subject matter of psychology: the “planets” are the gods, symbols of the powers of the unconscious.

In this same interview, Jung stated that the innate psychic predisposition of an individual “seems to be expressed in a recognizable way in the horoscope.” In many of his writings, Jung emphasized that astrology includes the sum total of all ancient psychological knowledge, including both the innate predisposition of individuals and an accurate way of timing crises.

In summary, astrology is admittedly hard to pin down, both scientifically and philosophically. But there is a richness to this ancient subject that, as shown in this chapter, has been broadly recognized as immensely valuable by some of the most brilliant minds in recorded
history. I maintain that it is particularly the association of astrology with
prediction
, in the minds of much of the public and also—unfortunately—in the claims of many practitioners of astrology, that has prevented many intelligent people from examining this profound and useful subject. Although many astrologers persist in the predictive attempts and methods of past centuries, fortunately a growing number of psychologically attuned, sophisticated modern practitioners no longer make such unqualified claims.

Medicine, economics, meteorology, and seismology are sciences that are difficult to pin down precisely and admittedly are not completely reliable. They are often therefore frustrating to those looking for certainty. And yet, few people object to these fields of study being referred to as legitimate sciences. Economics, in fact, is often called “the dismal science,” in part because economists so often make predictions that rarely work out in fact. But in the field of economics, as in astrology, if one stops asking for specific
predictions
, one finds that there are even more interesting things that one can learn and broader perspectives one can attain from those fields of study—especially about values, motivations, and human behavior.

In other words, one should approach each field of study and research
on its own terms
, and in such a way as to benefit from it as much as possible, rather than trying to find the imaginary perfect science. No other science has ever achieved any type of perfection or infallibility, and people in general do not expect that of medicine, meteorology, or economics. It is therefore not fair to demand that of astrology either.

Person-to-Person Astrology: Energy Factors in Love, Sex and Compatibility
