Love, Sex, and Relationships: Throughout History and Today

Love is a thing to be learned. It is a difficult, complex maintenance of individual integrity throughout the incalculable process of interhuman polarity.

—D. H. Lawrence

Love is an act of endless forgiveness, a tender look which becomes a habit.

—Peter Ustinov

We in the Western world live in a truly strange time, marked by widespread pride about our “freedom” and our supposed modern “knowledge,” but yet also imprisoned by our fears and insecurity and often made emotional invalids by our lack of self-knowledge. As the well-known critic of our current education system, Charles J. Sykes, wrote, “In place of a recognition that human life is marked by disappointment and limitation, we have enshrined the infinite expectation—for psychological gratification, self-actualization, self-realization, and happiness—not as a goal to be won, but as an entitlement” (
Dumbing Down Our Kids
, 1996).

The state of modern society and the pace of the modern lifestyle in the obsessive advertising-driven economy have created a disembodied
type of person, motivated by an incoherent mix of contradictory desires, opinions, and emotions. The confusion and state of being out of touch with one’s own body and deeper emotions (which are quite different from superficial, transitory, often self-indulgent “feelings”) all too often lead to our being unable to experience the “polarity” that is referred to above in D. H. Lawrence’s quote. The term “polarity,” apart from its electronic and magnetic definition in physical sciences, also denotes the opposite qualities and attunements in life, which are
complementary
 and which often stimulate each other—male/female, yin/yang, creative/receptive, and so forth. A Hopi Indian saying states, “Polarity is the loom upon which all reality is strung.” And if we cannot participate consciously and happily in that dance of polarities, we are truly missing much that life offers.

If we cannot experience polarity, we cannot experience dynamism in life, nor real creativity, deep excitement, or the satisfaction of a sense of completion, often kindled by encountering a complementary personality in a significant relationship. The importance given to and the attitude shown toward love and sex during any era is inextricably related to the social, psychological, and physical environment. But today in much of the Western world, the hectic pace of life, the ungrounded and transitory lifestyles, people’s rootlessness in the cities, and the extreme fads (and even psychic infections) that seem to spread ever more quickly internationally by electronic means all contribute to a feeling of disorientation and anxiety. People then grope for an experience of
grounding
 (or
polarity
), and since the essence of sex is polarization, many people try to find completion and satisfaction through sex alone. In fact, modern life in the West increasingly lacks concentration, relaxation, and polarization—all of which people often try to find through sex or sexually tinged relationships. But it should by now be obvious to anyone living in the Western world in recent years that Western society has overloaded sexuality with expectations, power, and commercial imagery that make it more and more
difficult to build and live honest, clear relationships based on one’s own instinctive needs and sensitivities. This issue will be further explored later in this chapter.

Some staggeringly incisive writings on this crucial area of modern life have been produced by Stanley Keleman, a therapist in the San Francisco Bay Area, whose work—it should be noted—has primarily been with North Americans and so is especially pertinent to that social and psychological milieu. His diagnoses of the delusions, self-deceptions, and deeper needs of modern people are tremendously thought-provoking and worth quoting here:

Levels of reality have become confused. In our attempts to create an egalitarian society, we have politicized and distorted gender. We live in a political climate that seeks to obliterate sexual differences, denouncing the concept of anatomy as destiny. By legislative fiat, society seeks to abandon maleness and femaleness, dismissing truths about differences and tensions known for ten thousand years of recorded history. This brings about an intellectual conception of equality, divorced from roots in experience or anatomy. (IDH, p. 58)

By seeking to obliterate obvious and natural differences between the sexes, we have also in effect diminished the experience of polarity that can fulfill, inspire, and heal. Keleman goes on to say,

In recent years, there has been an attack, both subtle and blatant, on the feelings and roles of male and female and on the place of family and commitment. Many people in our culture want to emphasize similarities and not differences. (IDH, p. 18)

There is public encouragement to seek satisfaction for past instinctual deprivations; to seek sexual equality instead of accepting sexual differentiation; to demand instant gratification and underplay loyalty, commitment, and struggle. The model is a mythic garden of
hedonistic leisure, where men and women demand all their political and biological rights as adults without having to work for them or develop the skills to create lasting relationships, emotional bonding, or family. Is it any wonder that people are confused about their sexuality, their roles, and their gender? (IDH, p. 24)

Because so many people today have lost touch with the simple basics of life and love, this book might be said to present a new key to understanding polarity. All of astrology, as we will see in forthcoming chapters, is based on polarities: the warming, energizing Sun and the cooling, soothing Moon; personal, harmonizing Venus and impersonal, aggressive Mars; the self-expressive energies of the Air and Fire elements, contrasted with the self-contained energies of the Earth and Water elements; and many others. In a sense,
astrology can be seen as a science/art of how human energies operate in daily life
, individually and interpersonally. Astrology can be not only illuminating for individuals seeking to know themselves and their life partners and patterns better, but also extremely useful as a corrective to balance the modern delusions and self-destructive concepts that seem increasingly to dominate our world. As Keleman writes,

When we disturb the procreative instincts and distort the meaning of gender, we diminish some of the deepest emotions and most selfless aspirations of which mankind is capable: the nurturing and love of children and the building of a more humane world for coming generations. (IDH, p. 8)

Historical and Cultural Background

Studying a variety of cultures and their history cannot help but reveal a stunning spectrum of modes of expressing affection and sexuality, as well as ways of constructing social institutions or acceptable roles for relationships between the sexes, raising children, and enhancing
social and intergenerational stability. Such studies also bring out the staggering variety of ways in which human beings have tried to understand the mysteries of intimate life and relations. It is widely known that the ancient Greeks had five different words for different types of “love.” Perhaps the most intriguing is
eros
, from which we get our word “erotic,” a much narrower term than the original from which it was derived.

The Greeks believed that continual regeneration of the self is inherent in eros, which urges one to seek a higher form of beauty, goodness, and truth. Their view was that the biological (physical sexuality) should be included but also transcended in eros. The god Eros was the child of Ares (Mars) and Aphrodite (Venus), polarized forces of masculine and feminine, sexuality and relatedness, strong assertiveness and beautiful attractiveness. Not only for the Greeks but also for many other later writers in a variety of cultures, eros was understood to drive us to transcend ourselves, to provide an urge for union and relatedness, and even a drive toward union with truth or God, as described by St. Augustine. Eros is the power that attracts us, draws us onward, whereas sex alone pushes us from behind. Eros refers to possibilities, but when seeking selfish satisfaction dominates, eros is eliminated. A Greek myth describes how Eros thrived and grew rapidly when he was with his brother Anteros, but he reverted to childish form when separated from him. This is an apt depiction of how, in the ancient Greek view, love (Eros) cannot grow without passion (Anteros), or at least a dynamic emotional or energy involvement.

In short, a broad view of the semidivine mystery of eros includes many of the subjects in this book and encompasses numerous aspects of life that have today unfortunately become fragmented, politicized, degraded, and separated from the rich texture of historical human experience and our deeper needs for intimacy. As Dr. Alice S. Rossi, a University of Massachusetts sociologist, wrote, “Modern society is a mere second in evolutionary history, and it is naïve to assume that our
audacious little experiments in … sex-role equality can overturn in a century, let alone in a decade, millennia of custom and adaptation.” In a similar vein, therapist Stanley Keleman wrote, “This fiddling around with sex roles has implications that have not been clearly articulated” (IDH, p. 73).

In fact, a wider acquaintance with the customs, traditions, and social forms across the world and throughout history would take a lot of the tension out of the overly rigid viewpoints so often heard today when discussing issues of love, sex, and gender roles. Such material should in fact form a large part of courses or programs that purport to teach “gender studies” or “feminism” or “human sexuality.” As Professor Zeldin writes, “… the history of love is not a sweeping movement towards greater freedom, but an ebb and flow, a whirlpool, and long periods of calm. The contracepted Westerners of today have many alternatives before them. It is surprising now that love is more highly valued than ever, that schools do not teach its history, its battles, the rise and fall of its dominions, its diplomatic methods and rhetoric, and the hypocrisy of its economics” (IHH, p. 76).

Marrying for love is a relatively new historical phenomenon and one that is predominantly found still mainly in the Western world. Most of the world’s marriages have been and continue to be arranged by parents or other elders for social and economic reasons. In most places and times, marrying for “love” has been seen as showing a lack of good sense. As Zeldin writes,

For most of history, love has been considered a threat to the stability of the individual and of society, because stability was usually valued more highly than freedom. In the 1950s still, only a quarter of engaged American couples said they were head over heels in love, and in France, less than a third of all women claimed to have experienced a
grand amour
. Forty years later, half of the French women were complaining that the men in their lives were not
romantic enough, and wishing they would at least say “I love you” more often. Their general view is that modern life makes amorous passions more difficult than in the past, but the golden age never existed. In despair, many of them say they feel more passionately about animals and sport than about people. In Russia, at the dawn of glasnost, even among newly-weds, love came only fifth in the list of the eighteen reasons for marrying. Which means that passion is an art that people have yet to master, that love is an unfinished revolution. (IHH, p. 83)

However, as art historian and social critic Camille Paglia writes, in spite of the sexual revolution and all the supposed freedom of emotional expression we now have, “What have we learned about ourselves? Practically nothing. Contrary to feminist propaganda, we have not found the answers to any important sexual issue. In fact, … we have barely begun to pose the questions correctly” (VT, p. 130). In addition, Paglia points out that arriving at satisfactory goals for human understanding and real equality between the sexes is actually inhibited by “this self-defeating tyranny of political correctness” (VT, p. 431). Zeldin likewise summarizes our inability to learn from previous generations’ experiences of love and sex: “But every generation, conscious only of how it differs from its parents and its children, forgets how old are most of the subjects of argument between men and women, how humans persist in banging their heads against the same wall” (IHH, p. 386).

Another theme running through history related to love and sex has been
idealization
 of the opposite sex or a specific partner, most commonly the woman. Such idealization, as Professor Zeldin points out, does not help us very much with really understanding our partners as they are, but it does offer a temporary romantic solution for loneliness and, during certain periods in history, a compensation for an oppressive awareness of human imperfection or sin. This tendency toward
idealization has been one more manifestation of eros through the ages and, in many instances, has resulted in producing profound religious devotion; uplifting artistic themes in poetry, sculpture, and painting; and even the code of chivalry, which added much to civility in interpersonal relations. The idealization of women is found in numerous branches of Hindu, Arab, and Christian cultural history.

Furthermore, idealization of the partner is apparent today even in this age of self-congratulatory supposed achievement of equality, realism, and legal rights between the sexes. But today, it seems often to take a neurotic form, such as fighting an inner battle between one’s idealized (but often unconscious) romantic emotions on one hand, and—on the other—the egocentric need for excessive self-respect, personal power, or uncompromising individual freedom. In fact, some of the types of idealization that confuse us or undermine our relationships today seem to grow from the egocentric culture of “infinite expectation” mentioned earlier in this chapter.

One manifestation of idealization seen in the Western world of collapsing families and marriages today, one that has generated millions of dollars for promoters of the concept, is that of “soul mates.” I won’t say much about this notion except that an accurate and incisive comparison of two people’s astrological charts can usually reveal whether sufficient real compatibility exists that they could even begin to consider themselves partners at such a level of intimacy. Looking at the comparison of charts can also, by the way, indicate to what extent idealization of one or both people is a major factor in the relationship, and also whether it is an inspiring, creative idealization or a seductive or self-deceptive glamorization—or a combination of both. (Particularly, Neptune interactions apply to this area of relationship analysis.)

The tragedy of allowing excessive or unhealthy idealization to run rampant—in our images of others, in our imaginings of what is possible in close relationships, or in our exalted concepts of our own perfection—is that it isolates dimensions of our life in a bubble that
then floats above, but is not linked to, the ground of practical or emotional reality. Indulging in a chronic habit of idealization (one that is permitted by an affluent leisure society and encouraged by many aspects of the therapy and self-help industries) can effectively seal off an individual from many of the deeper areas of personal experience that are enriching and sustainable. The refrain in the Leonard Cohen song “Anthem” poetically expresses the illuminating understanding and energy that can be experienced when one becomes reconciled to realistic limits in one’s relationships, something that is more likely to “ring a bell” with those over forty years of age.

Ring the bells that still can ring

Forget your perfect offering

There is a crack, a crack in everything

That’s how the light gets in.

(from
The Future
 album)

One often reads in Asian spiritual traditions that the mind is “the enemy of the real.” In the domain of relationships, the ideal is the enemy of the real. By this I do not mean to say that each person should not have individual life ideals. And certainly any relationship will have a far greater chance of prospering if both individuals share common spiritual and/or philosophical ideals. The practical “trade-offs” that must be accepted in relationships will be explored a bit more in later chapters. But one should keep in mind the guiding concept: with an ideal, you have perfection in your mind, but not necessarily any real life or vitality. With polarity, you may have conflict, but you also have energy flow and potential.

Although the cultural and historical variations in this field of intimate human activity are endlessly fascinating, there is no space in the scope of this book to explore them more fully. But I did want to establish this broad context not only to set the stage for the treatment of many archetypal dimensions of human nature in the later
chapters of this book, but also because I wanted to acknowledge briefly the vast spectrum of human behavior, images, and attitudes. Nevertheless, partly because there is today such a broad array of expectations, ideals, lifestyles, religious beliefs, and cultural traditions, after this chapter I will generally ignore most of today’s transitory fads, fashionable assumptions, contentious issues, and politically correct dogmas. Instead I want to focus on the essential energy attunement of individual human beings, a reality that is more lasting than much of what passes for fact in today’s media and academic infatuations. In addition, because the content of this book is relevant to people the world over, by no means do I want to narrow down what the science of astrology can offer only to the limited and fickle tastes of a few sectors of Western society.

Social Trends, Standards, and Hypocrisy

Even before the bitter gender politics of recent decades, the social fabric in many Western countries was already fraying; but the disintegration was hastened by numerous factors that today present a tremendous challenge to anyone trying to live a decent, stable life, particularly for those raising children. Divorce continues at an alarming rate, and the number of children involved in divorce has tripled in the past quarter century. One expert estimates that 45 percent of all American children born in any given year will live with only one of their parents at some time before they are eighteen. And more and more people are choosing to be single parents or wind up being one after a nonmarital relationship ends. No one can be sure what the ultimate impact of these trends will be, but many experts worry that instability in the family will ultimately make many social institutions unstable. As Albert Solnit, director of the Yale Child Study Center, said, “Divorce is one of the most serious and complex mental-health
crises facing children today.” If in fact astrology, or any other method, can help people to understand their relationships and partnership needs better, it is undoubtedly worthwhile and indeed imperative that it be examined and tried. After all, despite all the government programs, psychological theory, self-help books, and popularly available advice, the trends in most Western societies—it must be admitted—do not seem to be improving whatsoever.

One particularly revealing study, for example, should cause significant alarm about current societal trends: Harry Harlow, an animal behavior researcher, showed that monkeys, separated from their mothers, grew up with serious problems socially and sexually. They became violent and sexually confused about their roles; if impregnated, the females had hostile, destructive, murderous rages toward their offspring. In human societies, there is all too much evidence of the destructive effects of the absence of fathers as well. And one must wonder what sort of life and level of emotional health the future holds for children born today. For example, in the USA, married couples with children is a declining demographic category and now is less than 25 percent of all households.

Another worrying trend that has even begun to spread from the West to some conservative Asian societies is the degrading of public, as well as private, standards of behavior. Exhibitionism seems to be actually admired in some segments of societies, and many famous pop “artists” would be more accurately described as mass media sex performers. As Richard Tomkins points out in an article in
Financial Times
 (March 26, 2004), “Sexual imagery now permeates every aspect of mass culture, especially the media, advertising, fashion, entertainment and pop.” What he calls the “pornification” of society inevitably impacts intimate relationships of all kinds with violent ferocity, particularly relationships between young people and also between parents and children. Tomkins points out that, starting in the 1960s, the world
of commerce “seized upon and exploited” the new profit opportunities developed by the increasing sexual liberation and began to sell sexual images in a way never done before.

One serious repercussion of the increased marketing and promotion of sexual imagery is the schism that develops between one’s own intimate emotional and sexual nature, and the imagery promoted on all sides in the outside world. As Stanley Keleman writes, “When the media promote the idea that certain appearances and behaviors make a person desirable, sex becomes a fantasy divorced from our deepest urges. Sex becomes disembodied and exploitive” (IDH, p. 18). He continues,

Rather than learn from experience over time, we try to imitate instant images. When we are not encouraged to develop experiential references, but instead try to guide ourselves through images only, the emotional personality is underdeveloped. There is a confused avoidance of personal choice for the sake of a generalized, impersonal model. (IDH, p. 23)

The image of a repressed society is out of step with the truth. We are not a sexually repressive society; we are one that is overaroused and sensorily inflated. Overstimulation is our national crisis, not repression. Television, books, schools, radio, and newspapers compete for our attention, seeking access to our senses and internal lives. There is a mushrooming assault on our brains, our hormones, our bodies. (IDH, p. 44)

Please note especially the key phrase and insight that all these outside forces are
seeking access to our senses and internal lives!
 The reason I am covering a wide spectrum of facts about our society today and quoting from a range of experts and perceptive commentators, before getting into the details of astrology’s role in understanding
relationships, is that one has to
own
 (or
re-possess
) one’s internal life before one can have flourishing, mutually fulfilling relationships. And that is difficult in this day and age if one is not aware of the psychological and social influences and pressures that distort, manipulate, and undermine authentic relationship behavior. To put it another way, I am devoting some large sections of this book to these all-encompassing issues in order to give a realistic background for understanding our lives today and to describe, without varnishing the truth, the substantial hurdles that one has to surmount in order to have successful and satisfying relationships in the twenty-first century.

Keleman pulls no punches when he discusses the personal implications for each one of us of these trends that debase our sensitivities and ideals:

In the indiscriminate living out of sexual impulses, the opportunity to act from the messages of our gender and from the depth of emotional satisfaction is lost. Sex becomes an idealized activity exploited by external stimulation. We see this in the obvious overstimulation produced and demanded by our culture, in the uses of sex far removed from enriching personal maturation or forming enduring relationships. Sex has become a thing in itself and there is a constant seeking for variety, new techniques for arousal, and the substitution of fantasy for contact. Relationship is unimportant, a future is unimportant. Sex is done for sex itself. This is not freedom, but a misdirected use and outcome of contraception. (IDH, p. 75)

A wide range of conflicts, double-bind situations, and hypocrisies arise from the trends just described. One is the contradiction between the professed desires of many women for respect and equality, and their dress, behavior, and intentionally projected public image. Men the world over have noticed this contradiction, yet so many women disown any responsibility in this area of life. This denial is often
perplexing to men, but perhaps it is explained by the following observation by Emma Jung, the lifelong wife of the renowned psychologist C. G. Jung:

For by her unconsciousness, woman exerts a magical influence on man, a charm that lends her power over him. Because she feels this power instinctively and does not wish to lose it, she often resists to the utmost the process of becoming conscious. (E. Jung,
Animus and Anima
, Continuum International, 1985)

It seems clear to most men that female provocation through inappropriate dress and behaviors should be considered just as much a type of sexual harassment as are the various forms of male aggressive suggestiveness. It is difficult for many men in positions of authority to take seriously a complaint of a “glass ceiling” against the promotion of women when it comes from someone whose dress and occasional behavior is reminiscent of a streetwalker or a flirtatious teenager, and thus who seems to have little awareness of others or sense of responsibility. And as Stanley Keleman puts it so clearly,

The confusion is worsened by the sexual fashions that assail us from all directions. Provocative displays mean look and admire, but do not touch. Clothing that accentuates the organs of sexual arousal does not reflect the real meaning of sexual satisfaction. Indeed, not only does it produce frustration and the attendant unwelcome responses, but it is an act of violence against the true functioning of anatomy. We allow ourselves to be overstimulated, while at the same time abiding by social demands for restraint and self-control. Little wonder, then, that a permissive culture witnesses within itself a drastic and escalating increase in all manner of violence. (IDH, p. 48)

Social and Sexual Roles and Reality

The wisest and most loving teacher I have tried to learn from in my life once said, “Where there is love, there is no question of rights and equality.” I do not know why anyone would not accept that as an ideal for
all
 human relationships of every type, although I am sure there are many who would object and consider me a hopelessly romantic idiot. But remember that I stated that as an
ideal
. As a person with a Libra Sun (the sign of the balance or scales of justice), I appreciate the balance of complementary functions of the sexes in daily living, as well as their complementary psychological natures. And the ideal of a fair and balanced harmony between people is admittedly attractive to me, and—I would expect—to the majority of other people. So perhaps it is time to examine some of the trends and viewpoints of modern society that inhibit the development of such harmony. And, while doing so, it is important to acknowledge the real function of social and sexual roles in the broadest and most realistic way we can.

In an effort to balance the scales of commentary devoted to such subjects, I fully disclose my emphasizing the male point of view in this section of the book, not pretending to be able to do otherwise, as a number of male psychologists I have met pretend to do. I actually feel that they are being patronizing to women and unfair to men, not to mention hypocritical, by adopting the jargon of “sisterhood,” “victimization,” and “empowerment,” when challenging clients to think in new ways would perhaps be more beneficial. Perhaps the fact that most of their clients are women has motivated them to adopt this inauthentic persona and attitude and even sometimes to misrepresent the nature of most men to their female clients.

I also feel that women’s point of view has certainly had a fair hearing in recent years, and the feminist point of view has been publicized everywhere—especially that of the loudest and angriest of
such commentators. That was no doubt necessary for a time, but that time has surely ended in much of the Western world. As psychiatrist Eleanor Bertine, MD, writes regarding extreme versions of feminism in her excellent book
Jung’s Contribution to Our Time
, “We can forgive them for their one-sidedness, for all movements have to be one-sided in order to be effective. However, the attempt to erase the specific qualities of each sex and to pare woman down to being a less efficient but more heavily burdened breed of man pleased the women no more than it did the men. Psychologically, the only real contentment comes from fulfilling one’s inherent potentialities, not from distorting oneself to imitate somebody else” (p. 135).

The last sentence in the above quote again reminds us of how
authentic
 self-expression is distorted by too much adherence to abstract concepts, social fads, or extreme “isms”—as we have seen throughout the twentieth century from the distortions of personality and individual integrity exhibited by so many people in regimes and social situations where they felt intimidated. But society as a whole has also paid a price for unthinkingly appeasing gender extremists by too readily adapting to their demands. Europe would do well to carefully analyze the actual results of the USA’s poorly thought out and poorly planned all-too-eager appeasement of gender activists. There has, for example, been a huge social, emotional, legal, and economic price paid for the excessive flirtation with extreme feminist notions. As an experienced woman entrepreneur, Sarah McCarthy pointed out in an opinion piece in
Forbes
 magazine that this “cultural fascism” has in fact trivialized the women’s movement. She points out that the extreme approach has alienated women’s natural allies and made men and women who want to work “shoulder to shoulder” instead occupy themselves with looking
over their shoulders
, in fear of lawsuits or at least harassment accusations; and she states that innocent games, nuances, and light flirting that used to
alleviate
 workplace tension have now been made into criminal activity. Another important point she makes is no small matter:

I am a feminist, but the law has made me fearful of hiring women. If one of our cooks or managers … offends someone, it could cost us $100,000 in punitive damages and legal expenses. To the leadership of the women’s movement: You do women a disservice.… you are frightening managers into hiring men over women. I know that I am so frightened. (“In My Opinion,”
Forbes
, December 9, 1991)

One of the most illuminating and profound insights expressed by therapist Stanley Keleman is: “The importance of roles is that they are conduits and passageways for the parts that nature intends us to live” (IDH, p. 63). And to take the social and sexual roles that have developed in human society over thousands of years and reject them completely is to deny the reality of human beings and their societies in a way that has a profound impact that we cannot even begin to imagine. It seems time to face up to certain realities before societies in the West try to mold themselves according to an imaginary concept of “person” that is at best merely a recent experiment. Once again, Keleman illuminates this issue:

Gender is the ingredient that determines the relationship between the sexes, and it is being challenged by today’s concept of the “person.” The person is supposed to be free of maleness or femaleness, a sort of ideal ego state, lacking biological and emotional gender. This notion of the person has entered the political, legal, and judicial areas, where equality is meant to overcome discrimination according to gender. The idea of equal opportunity within our laws and institutions has been extended, and has come to be interpreted to mean that males and females are equal biological and emotional beings. We have created a mythological, nonsexed person with no base in biological reality, derived only from political, legal, and sexual fantasy. (IDH, p. 72)

By accepting the dogmas of extremist gender role advocates, we are creating more and more misunderstanding and doing a disservice to
both sexes and to society as a whole. When women accept extreme opinions from the loudest and most aggressive “feminists,” they then force themselves to build up many of the qualities that they have come to dislike in many men, and they often destroy, deny, or devalue the best qualities that most women have expressed quite naturally. While many women in the West are admittedly growing past an outmoded narrow role, men are just beginning to do so, usually without much help from women. Many studies have shown how much men are oppressed physically, socially, and psychologically by an outmoded sexual and social role in many countries. For example, one might read
The Myth of Male Power
 by Warren Farrell, Ph.D., an extraordinary source of hard-to-find facts. The author is in fact the former elected male board member of the National Organization for Women in New York City, which he quit when he became convinced that that form of feminism was “gender politics’ one-party system.”

Many of the ideas, accusations, and even institutional policies created by extreme sex role activism have forced men to fight back (often reluctantly) after being on the defensive for years and allowing a great deal of propaganda and intolerance to affect their minds and relationships. Frankly, most men do not want to
compete
 with women. They
need
 women, seemingly in a way and at a level of intensity that women often don’t understand. (See the next section of this chapter for more details.) But after being forced into an endless number of double-bind situations and after becoming sick of the inauthentic and often hypocritical behavior that was demanded of them, men of many ages and social and educational levels are saying, “Enough!”

What are these “double-binds”? I will itemize a few of them as follows:

The “modern” woman says she wants men to exhibit “softer” qualities, freedom in emotional expression, gentleness, and vulnerability. But men trying to open themselves to these repressed parts of
themselves report that women lose respect for them if they are not “strong and in control”—traditional male characteristics.

Following from the above observation, a survey revealed that most top male executives have nonworking wives, often a necessity in order to manage a complex lifestyle. Yet virtually all top female executives have husbands who work full-time. It is interesting to speculate whether the woman would respect him if he did not also work.

There are severe problems in trying to be “whole” men because the business world is still oriented toward an unyielding work ethic that largely doesn’t recognize personal needs, emotional expression, or the uncertainties of men in lifestyle transitions. (Women who enter this “man’s world,” rather than changing it, seem to adapt themselves to the typical male work ethic of sacrificing for the company by working long hours.)

While many men are turning away from the emotionless charade in which sex is a game of conquest, more and more women are rejecting serious commitment, as well as adopting the old “male” approach of testing their powers of conquest.

Many men have tremendous difficulty giving each other emotional support. In contrast to the support networks women build, many men find it difficult even to be real friends with each other; men who believe they have genuine male friendships concede that there is often a barrier to discussing many personal topics.

Many women say automatically when asked that they want love or deep companionship in a relationship, but more in-depth research and confidential surveys often reveal other motives, such as money, security, status, children, regular sex, etc. Men who are romantic but also gullible and take women’s assertions at face value are often
left stunned and disillusioned when they experience this two-faced behavior, as many therapists can confirm. (Admittedly, this does seem to be a parallel to the common male behavior so often decried by women of promising anything to get sex!)

As sexual freedom has expanded, more wide-ranging behaviors have been explored by women; but many men feel that there has not been a parallel increase in women’s interpersonal honesty about their desires. Most women still expect men to initiate sex, but yet somehow to guess how to do it in a way the woman might prefer. If a man is forthright and candid about his passions or lust, although some will welcome such honesty, most women still consider that improper and instinctively act insulted or indignant, responding with anger, tears, or other signs of disappointment. But in spite of this distaste for blunt honesty, counselors and therapists today hear so much from women about their not being sexually satisfied and their not finding men who are really aggressive or “potent.”

The narrowness of the male role and the built-in double-binds also have another impact that is especially relevant to this book. It is well known that women are far more open to subtler, intuitive, and inexplainable but valid areas of human experience. Astrology is studied and used and valued by a much greater number of women—to whom its accuracy is starkly obvious—than men. However, this is partly a cultural phenomenon and thus related to social roles. For example, in much of Asia, the men are the most serious and scholarly students of astrology and constitute the majority of its practitioners. However, in most of the Western world, the male role has traditionally been linked with the “logical,” analytic mind and also with “science” in its most rigid sense, rather than as an objective methodology. This has caused many men to fear the “irrational,” which not only puts a severe limitation on the areas of life they will consider worth exploring, but it also limits their ability to relate to others and to their own inner life. Many
men in the West lack what is called “intuition,” the ability to sense a situation’s meaning or potential, as well as the natural instinct to trust their responses when the clues are not at all visible. It is undoubtedly time for men to break out of such a restrictive role, for it affects not just relationships, but also intellectual breadth, creativity, emotional expression, and physical health.

Men’s Little-Appreciated Need for Women

Enough material has been published about male and female roles in the past two decades to fill a huge library. But much less research and perceptive commentary is available regarding how the sexes view each other or try to understand the other’s needs for intimate relationship with the opposite sex. For example, few women seem to have any idea how desperately men need women’s company, support, and—dare I say it—femininity. The support for this observation, with examples throughout history, would take five hundred pages, but I will mention a few widely agreed-on attitudes and a few quotations.

Few women understand the
violence
 of men’s sexuality and attraction to women—its intensity, suddenness, and obsessive, persistent nature. That many women are oblivious to this fact of life is shown not only by many naïve statements and expectations that women (including feminists) express, but perhaps most of all by the style of dress that many women take pains to adopt. As Robert A. Johnson writes, “Very few women understand how great is the hunger in a man to be near femininity” (
SHE: Understanding Feminine Psychology
, p. 27). More than two hundred years ago, the great French writer Montaigne elaborated on this with surprising bluntness: “The lustful longing which allures us to women seeks but to expel that pain which an earnest and burning desire doth possess us with, and desireth but to allay it thereby to come to rest and be exempted from this fever” (
Essays
).

It is one of the worst aspects of some modern societies that the
increasing demands and pressures in daily life force everyone, including women and often even children, to be more and more competitive; but that very quality makes the women less and less complementary with the men, who continue to need them more than they can say. It is obvious that much better and deeper communication is needed between the sexes, not only to understand each other, but also to acknowledge their need of each other. As Nathaniel Branden writes,

In my experience, much of the so-called war of the sexes is a result of a fear of rejection, abandonment, or loss. Often men and women experience great resistance to accepting how much they need each other, how important the opposite sex is for the enjoyment of life and the fulfillment of their own masculine or feminine potentialities. Often there is almost hatred of the fact that we need the opposite sex as much as we do. (
The Psychology of Romantic Love
)

Surveys and Silliness: How We Understand Sex, Love, and Relationships Today

For many years, I have felt that most of the modern studies in this field had gigantic gaps and indeed lacked the human depth needed to resonate with people’s experience. Many of the teachings, research, counseling approaches, and surveys seemed so abstract and mechanical that they left me feeling like they were talking about a futuristic society of robots or zombies who have no feelings, social traditions, religious values, spiritual ideals, or emotional yearnings. The depersonalized depiction of intimate life that resulted was not only offensive and ridiculous but also completely inaccurate. When personal, sexual, and family life standards were in the hands of priests and ministers in past eras, and when people looked to them for authoritative advice, that was a kind of limitation; but people expected it and often compensated for it! But when people today look to the modern
priesthood of psychotherapists and the proponents of an unquestioning belief in “science,” they are left with another form of limitation, one that leaves out far too much of the richness of life and offers us little promise of a happy and fulfilling future. And we are just beginning to realize the limitations of those approaches and to learn how to compensate—often by ignoring much of the information promoted in the media!

As Keleman wrote,

Therapists and counselors too often support these notions by encouraging uncommitted sexuality and sex for easy gratification, discounting by omission its seriousness and mystery. Little or nothing is said about the value of restraint and maturity, or of the power inherent in the emotional nature of sexuality, or the struggles that are part and parcel of heterosexual bonding. The values of family and long-term relationship are too parochial, restrictive, or boring. The dramatic increase of single-parent families is testimony to this modern version of freedom. Not only are there fewer and fewer models for successful heterosexual bonding, but we, as a culture, are moving farther away from the enduring truths that heterosexual interaction has to teach. (IDH, p. 7)

The media today are filled with surveys that are statistical and thus purport to be “scientific,” but their basis and their bias are rarely examined. Those who agree to spend their time responding to questions about any subject are obviously more focused on that subject than other people. Those who volunteer to become part of surveys about intimate life are people for whom such issues are more important than they are for the public at large. Furthermore, they are people who do not mind revealing details of their private life to strangers, hence defining themselves as not at all “average” people. Thus we cannot assume that they are able to speak for others. In fact, they are not representative of the “average” person whatsoever. The approach taken
in such surveys is often completely mechanical, as if people were all the same model of the same machine, with few individual differences in emotion, sensitivity, intensity, needs, tastes, or values. How absurd! As Keleman wrote,

There is professional literature on sexuality that explains sex as a mechanical function, no more than stimulus-response. It is as if human sexuality functioned like a machine, to be wired to perform this way or that. Too many current approaches to sexuality deliberately and continually divorce it from the emotional development of the individual. There is a concentration on technique and performance, on descriptions of sexuality as mechanical programs, orgasmic catharsis, or narcissistic fulfillment. (IDH, p. 18)

Even though sexual and relationship surveys like Shere Hite’s books provide useful information about the varieties of human nature and behavior, they still give no clues about those things that astrology can clarify, including:

An understanding of compatibility at many specific levels

Methods for identifying a person whose energy attunement is complementary to one’s own

Deeper insight into why one feels and thinks and needs as one does

In assembling the data and material for this book, I was well aware of all the above issues. No doubt the more mental types of people who enjoy analyzing their intimate lives responded the most to my extensive and detailed questionnaires. (We would call them Mercurian types in astrology.) But I have sought to compensate for that by selecting only the particularly insightful responses for this book, rather than using a statistical method, and also by drawing on many case histories, as well as doing many interviews at my initiative and with volunteers. In
fact, I often found myself interrogating many people quite thoroughly, even those who somewhat resisted my questioning. In any case, I am comfortable that the direct quotes from people in later sections of this book comprise a significant and fair-minded contribution to the accuracy of the material and overall conclusions presented.

Person-to-Person Astrology: Energy Factors in Love, Sex and Compatibility

4

Astrology in the Twenty-First Century: A New Viewpoint

Where is the wisdom
We have lost in knowledge?
Where is the knowledge
We have lost in information?

—T. S. Eliot

A decade ago it was often said that the amount of information available to us doubled every five years. Who knows how much faster the information explosion is moving now, what with the Internet’s expansion and the existence of thousands of databases? However, the ratio of information
used
 to information available is decreasing daily! And undoubtedly the ratio of
useful
 information readily available must be at an all-time historical low. With the widespread decline of academic, scholarly, and journalistic standards and the broad rebellion against all forms of authority, globally augmented by the Internet, the analogy that the Internet is a vast museum that has no curator seems more and more apt.

And yet, with all the availability of information and pseudo-knowledge, anyone in search of deeper understanding of human life and peace of mind might well ask the same questions that the great
poet T. S. Eliot posed in the above quotation: where is the wisdom? In other words, what can give us real understanding and insight into our individual situations and our society’s interpersonal and cultural predicaments? We should be ready to admit that the trends of the last few decades have failed to provide such perceptive solutions, and therefore we need to try a new approach that is
holistic
, that integrates open-mindedly the broad variety of human enterprises and modes of exploring human nature. The artificial distinctions of the past must be rejected if they interfere with our search for
beneficial
 methods and practical results.

As the philosopher-historian Dr. Vartan Gregorian, former President of the New York Public Library, stated:

I worry that the search for technological progress is no longer a means, but has become an end in itself, escaping from human control and thus dominating man, alienating us from ourselves, our society and our environment.… We witness a growing and, in my opinion, an artificial and unnecessary gulf between the humanities and the sciences.… I believe in the
re-integrating
 of knowledge, if we as a society are to survive.… technology is a cultural artifact, something we have created. It is a means to an end, and we must decide upon what that end is to be. We must control and channel it or it will lead us in the wrong direction. (from article in
Publishers Weekly
 magazine, May 30, 1986)

In a number of his books, Arthur Koestler, whose impressive and varied work I described in
Chapter 2
, points to a way to bridge the destructive and unnecessary gap between the quantitative and qualitative approaches to understanding life. He points out that a blend of curiosity and wonder (the mental and the emotional components) “jointly motivate the scientist’s and the artist’s voyages of exploration.” In fact, it would go a long way toward cooperation between many different disciplines, professions, and factions of society if we all
would view the diversity of approaches taken as authentic “voyages of exploration” worthy of respect. Koestler provides a graph (see
Figure 4.1
), which I have taken the liberty of altering slightly, including the addition of Astrology, in its role as a method for exploring human personality and experience, interactions with others, and life’s energies and cycles, based on its unique combination of both quantitative and qualitative dimensions.

Figure 4.1

Koestler says that this diagram from his book
The Act of Creation
 was meant to illustrate the continuity of the “two cultures” (the humanities and the sciences) and the broad spectrum of many fields of study:

The vertical axis represents the dimension of “Objective Truth,” verifiable by experiment, the horizontal axis represents the subjective experience of “Beauty”; the former governed by the intellect,
the latter by the emotions. The chart shows neighboring provinces of science and art arranged in a continuous series. As we move downwards along the sloping curve, “objective verifiability” steadily diminishes, while the subjective, emotional factor increases. Yet even in the highest ranges of the curve, the realm of pure mathematics, we find disturbing logical paradoxes which cannot be resolved, and mathematicians yearning for “beauty in the equations.” As we descend from mathematics to sub-atomic physics, the interpretations of empirical data become more and more controversial; and so we can move further down the slope, from the so-called exact sciences like chemistry through biology, then through medicine—which is, alas, a much less exact science—through such hybrid domains as psychology and biography to the biographical novel and lastly into pure fiction.… The point of this game is to show that regardless of what scale of values you choose to apply, you move through a continuum, without encountering a crack in the Grecian urn—or a precipice between the two cultures. (AOC, p. 372)

There are numerous ways to describe astrology, but in the context of Koestler’s diagram, it might be appropriate to call astrology a type of
mathematical biography
 when it is applied to understanding individual human beings. This is a fitting term because astrology includes an examination of both one’s inner and outer life, but based upon a foundation of precise measurements of the planets—their location in the heavens and their geometric relationships to each other and to the earth. In fact, a birth chart (or
horoscope
, to use the ancient Greek term) is simply a map of the heavens as observed from the earth, at the exact moment of birth and from the exact location of the birth. It was particularly striking to me, shortly after I thought of the term “mathematical biography,” when I recalled that the great philosopher-composer-astrologer Dane Rudhyar (1895–1990) called astrology “an algebra of life” as early as 1936. That description appeared in his
pioneering book
The Astrology of Personality
, the first modern integration of sophisticated astrology with depth psychology. This profoundly influential book is still in print today and has sold hundreds of thousands of copies in many languages.

Another simple way to approach astrology, especially for those new to the subject, is to see it as just an extension and broadening of our everyday reality. It simply deals with time and planetary cycles (including
earth cycles
). Everyone experiences the reality of a day (the earth’s revolution about its axis), a month (approximating a complete lunar cycle), and a year (a complete revolution of the earth around the sun). Everyone sees (and in fact cannot fail to see) the obvious correlations of our everyday experience with these solar system cycles. In order to be able to relate to the fundamental basis of astrology, therefore, one simply has to extend the boundary of his or her consciousness beyond the orbit of the earth and its moon, into other dimensions of life. One has only to tune in to the
other
 cycles within our solar system by acknowledging that they also have a place in the functional ecology of the entire system. Admittedly, the Earth, Moon, and Sun cycles and rhythms have the most
obvious
 links with experiences that are common to all human beings. But is it not reasonable to assume that the other planets as well might have similar links with our lives, albeit more subtle and harder to perceive with the physical senses?

In fact, more and more discoveries and models created by contemporary science point to the inevitable unity and interlinking of all of creation and all dimensions of life. And as time goes on, the quantitative and the qualitative approaches are appearing less and less estranged. Koestler explained in
The Sleepwalkers
 how the quantitative approach for some time threatened to obliterate all other ways of understanding life:

Galileo’s “the book of nature is written in the language of mathematics” was regarded by his contemporaries as a paradox; today
it has become unquestioned dogma. For a long time the reduction of quality to quantity—of colour, sound, radiation to vibrational frequencies—was so eminently successful that it seemed to answer all questions. But when physics approached the ultimate constituents of matter, quality took its revenge: the method of reduction to quantity still worked, but we no longer know just what it is that is being thus reduced. (SWK, p. 544)

In other words, the quantitative approach, which insists on everything in life being measurable to be considered
real
, has reached the extreme, paradoxical point where we are forced to recognize again the reality and profound value of universal qualities, principles, virtues, and realms of experience that are absolutely immeasurable. I emphasize here that astrology itself is an integrated and synthesizing system that
incorporates both the quantitative and the qualitative;
 for it is a language of qualitative descriptions and distinctions, based on a foundation of precise mathematical measurements of the solar system.

In the last few decades, there has been an explosion of new ideas and models that synthesize the old dichotomies over which people have fruitlessly argued for so long. The rigid old categories of “science” have disintegrated, leaving us room to breathe intellectually, emotionally, and spiritually. In fact, we should realize that scientific “truth” is
always
 expressed in terms of the worldview of the times. Therefore, the current scientific version of truth or reality will inevitably change and in fact may well be completely discarded in the future. Any honest person who is acquainted with the history of human thought must acknowledge that there is probably as much superstition and misunderstanding embedded in modern science’s assertions as in the ancient sciences and myths.

Laurence L. Cassidy, Ph.D., SJ, a scientist who also studies and uses astrology, wrote in an article that many theories in physics now suggest that communication within the universe takes place instantaneously.
Explaining why
all
 the planets in our solar system are relevant to human lives, no matter how distant from the earth, he continues, “… spatial distance makes no difference in determining cause and effect” (CSA).

Lynne McTaggart’s popular book
The Field
 summarizes a great deal of recent research in science and the healing arts that also points to this emerging unity in how we conceptualize life’s workings and our own nature. This book states, “A substructure underpins the universe that is essentially a recording medium of everything, providing a means for everything to communicate with everything else” (TF, p. 294).
The Field
 further concludes that “people are indivisible from their environment. Living consciousness is not an isolated entity” (p. 294). Much of the book reports on evidence that demolishes old notions of separateness, time and space limitations, and distinct material forms. McTaggart asserts that “we would be viewed more as a complex network of energy fields in some sort of dynamic interplay …” (p. 14). She further concludes, “You could only understand the universe as a dynamic web of interconnection … through all space and time” (ibid.). Such terminology is remarkably similar to some of the energy language with which I described astrology thirty years earlier, in my book
Astrology, Psychology and the Four Elements: An Energy Approach to Astrology & Its Use in the Counseling Arts
. It is also similar to the results of research into electromagnetic fields done for many decades by Robert Becker, MD, an orthopedic surgeon and professor. Dr. Becker, in his book
The Body Electric
, notes that even very small magnetic fields have a significant influence and that “Life’s geomagnetic coupling to heaven and earth is apparently more like a web than a simple cord and socket.”

In relation to astrology, these contemporary insights and holistic perceptions about the nature of life and the universe open the door for even the most skeptical and rationalistic person to take a serious new look at astrology. And they also enable astrologers (and researchers,
doctors, and therapists who use astrology perhaps somewhat discreetly) to more fully embrace the unified view of life that astrology embodies and
helps us explore
. As Dr. Laurence Cassidy writes,

So far as we astrologers are concerned, all of this releases great drafts of fresh air. First of all, there are, literally, no legitimate grounds within the scientific community’s self-understanding for a denunciation of astrology as false. It may well be the case that our practice does not fit within the limits of “science” as the group consensus currently understands the term, but since almost no one within that community seriously maintains that “scientific method” is the only path to truth, there is no logical reason why a scientist cannot also accept the truth of astrology, just as he can enjoy poetry or be a learned theologian. Different disciplines have different methodologies and there is no reason to condemn the hand because it is not the foot. (CSA)

After all, we should never forget that all sorts of fields of study merely create
models
, which are only useful for a time and do not represent any ultimate reality. They are inevitably replaced by other models some time later. Behind these limited human models lies an inconceivable reality, of which only a part can be comprehended by our rational mind or scientific systems. The real value of such models, at least in fields related to human psychological and physical health, can be determined mainly by their utility in creating beneficial results, more positive behavior, better health, or greater understanding or tolerance. And different fields of study can have significantly different models or conceptualizations of the same thing. For example, consider the following two ways of describing the Sun, which one could say concisely portrays the very division between people who consider astrology to be a valuable study and those who consider themselves “scientific” in the old sense of the term:

One can, indeed, speak of the Sun as a vast collection of hydrogen gas, and there will be some significance in that statement, to the extent that one comprehends both chemistry and spectroscopic analysis. But one can also speak of the Sun as the symbol of an individual’s ego and/or as the light of one’s life, and this will also be meaningful to the extent that one understands spiritual psychology. One could argue that the second usage is merely metaphorical or symbolic, and there is a sense in which that is true, but then the designation of the Sun as a vast ball of hydrogen gas is no less metaphorical and/or symbolic. Possibly the main way in which post-Renaissance Science has managed to deceive itself and many others is the naïve way in which it ascribes a position of privilege to its metaphors vis-à-vis other metaphors. (CSA)

Since
all
 models are simply models, not an expression of ultimate truth, and so destined to decay over time as human understanding and consciousness develop, I think it is time for intelligent people to assume more perspective and broader objectivity when various models seem to clash. One model of our solar system that I find useful for understanding astrology is described by Jeffrey Goodman in his book
We Are the Earthquake Generation
. He outlined the views of one particularly innovative theorist:

 … the laws of magnetism are even more important [than the laws of gravity]. He pictured the solar system as a giant electromagnetic field, in which lines of magnetic force stretch out between the sun and the planets. He said that the planetary system is carefully balanced and each planet acts like a magnetic gyrocompass, where the sudden deviation of one planet almost immediately and very directly affects all the other planets through this magnetic field. Thus … small planets, which have small gravitational effects, could nevertheless have large (magnetically derived) effects on the other planets when they were located at pivotal positions. (p. 189)

Please note the statement that a “deviation of one planet almost immediately and very directly affects all the other planets through this magnetic field.” Not only does this view of the solar system allow us to do away with rigid space and time concepts as limitations to planetary “influence”—just as astrology implicitly postulates—but it also helps to explain how the distant planets Neptune and Pluto have been correlated with such immense “effects” in astrology. This model of our solar system as a self-sustaining energy system, perhaps somewhat like an electro-magnetic dynamo, is in fact in accord with many recent scientific theories. It is also useful on a practical level for practitioners of astrology trying to understand the various energy cycles and major changes in individual lives that so often are otherwise unexplainable.

So what kind of astrology is now available for the twenty-first century? What is its value, what are its applications, and what should it be based upon? My previous books have included hundreds of pages on those complex subjects, but here let me just emphasize a few points. The modern type of astrology to which I am referring is:

Based on empirical observation as well as on traditional meanings of the planetary symbols

Verified through continual experiential proofs and feedback with people

Used within a discipline of clear basic principles

Absolutely open to continuous refinement and development through research and discovery

I have for years witnessed the inherent power of understanding and healing that astrology can provide,
if communicated effectively
 in a clear, direct language. Indeed, astrology is the very all-inclusive framework that the broad field of psychology desperately needs to clarify the
dynamic energies and life principles that enliven individual personalities. Using astrology intelligently greatly facilitates self-acceptance. In addition, it reveals that there
are
 understandable cycles in individual lives that—once we are aware of them—can be taken advantage of or adjusted to.

Importantly, astrology shows the objective reality of each individual personality (of which the person may be only somewhat conscious) before the social pressures, cultural influences, and type of upbringing made their impact. In other words, the birth chart provides a symbolic map of our deeper nature, before it was colored by many overlays. Insecurity has become the most common complaint of our time, but to attain a sense of security one must become strongly established within oneself through some kind of inner certainty and/or self-knowledge. Astrology is one significant tool for achieving this kind of self-understanding and inner security, and its helpfulness in clarifying the compatibility issues of relationships further aids both self-acceptance and the attainment of more satisfying interpersonal experience. As we will see in the next chapters, using astrology unlocks many of the confusing mysteries of interpersonal relationships, as well as clarifies one’s own individual relationship needs.
